「他惦记他那几匹马，精得很，」老头回到座上说；「不过俺寻思，有时候有人比他还精哩。」

「你说的是谁？」他的老搭子赫奇斯农夫问。

「啊，走着瞧。俺有半个心眼儿想告诉他；可他那种人，拿俺们的话不当回事。」

===Original===
“Not a mossel of use,” said Hedges sententiously. “These yer quality be such a akkerd lot;” and he knocked the ashes out of his pipe on the iron-bound edge of the trestle-table. The object of this armour was to prevent the labourers sticking their billhooks into it when they called for a quart, for hedge-cutters are apt to strike their tools into the nearest piece of wood when they want their hands free. Having filled the pipe again, and finding he had no match, he stepped into the inn-kitchen to light it at the fire, and instantly noticed a large red-hot nail in a log of burning wood.

“Missis, missis!” The landlady came running. “Look ee thur﻿—thur’s a crooked nail in thuck log. Draw un out﻿—doan’t ee waste un. Nails be amazin’ useful thengs.”

“Zo um be,” said Farmer Ruck. “Volk used to save um. I knowed them as had a gallon measure full of hoss-stubs: thaay be the toughest iron, and makes the vinest gun-barrels.”

“Them cut nails be as rotten as matchwood,” said Bill the “wunt-catcher,” i.e. mole-catcher, throwing down his wooden traps. “Time o’ day to ee, missus;” nodding to her over the mug, and meaning good health. “The vinest gun as ever you seed wur thuck long un up to Warren. Mebbe you minds Kippur Mathew?”

“I minds un,” said Farmer Ruck.
===Chinese===
「一星半点儿用也没有，」赫奇斯煞有介事地说。「这帮上等人，别扭得很；」说着他在支架桌包铁的桌沿上磕出烟斗里的灰。桌沿包铁，是为了防雇工们来打一夸脱酒时把钩刀戳进桌面——割篱人要腾出手来的时候，总爱把家什扎进手边最近的一块木头。重新装满烟斗，一摸没带火柴，他便踱进酒馆厨房凑火点烟，一眼瞥见一块烧着的木柴里有根烧红的大铁钉。

「老板娘，老板娘！」老板娘跑了过来。「恁瞧那儿——那块柴里有根弯钉子。拔出来——可别糟蹋了。钉子这物件，用处大着哩。」

「可不是，」拉克农夫说。「早年间人家都攒着哩。俺认得有人攒了一加仑斗的旧马蹄钉：那是顶韧的铁，打的枪管顶好。」

「那些机制切钉，烂得跟火柴杆儿似的，」捕鼹人比尔（Bill）——所谓「捕地耗子的」，也就是捕鼹人——撂下他的木头夹子说道。「老板娘，问恁好；」隔着酒杯朝她点头，意思是祝她康健。「恁这辈子见过顶好的枪，就是沃伦（Warren）那杆长的。恁还记得看守马修（Mathew）不？」

「记得哩，」拉克农夫说。

===Original===
“Thuck gun would kill your chain. Thur wur a hole in the barrel as yer med put yer vinger in. Mathew, he squints along at the geame, and I holds a dock-leaf1 auver this yer hole, and he lets vly, and kills half a score o’ quests,” (wood-pigeons).

“A’ wur a chap to fiddle,” said Hedges. “A’ made hisself a fiddle out o’ thuck maple as growed in Little Furlong hedge. Hulloa, Pistol-legs!”

This was addressed to an aged man who had crawled up on two sticks. His legs, bent outwards﻿—curved like the butt of a pistol﻿—had obtained him this nickname.

“Nation dry weather,” said the ancient, lifting his head with some difficulty. “Gie me a drap.”

A labourer leaning against the elm handed him his quart.
===Chinese===
「那杆枪，隔着锁链都能打穿。枪管上有个窟窿，手指头都塞得进。马修眯起眼瞄那猎物，俺就揪片酸模叶1捂住这个窟窿，他一放枪，撂倒十来只『咕咕』，」（就是林鸽）。

「他是个拉提琴的好手，」赫奇斯说。「他自己做了把提琴，用的是小弗隆（Little Furlong）树篱里长的那棵枫树。喂，手枪腿！」

这话是对一个拄着两根棍子挪过来的老头说的。他的两条腿往外弯——弯得像手枪把——这绰号便由此得来。

「天干得邪乎，」老人家费劲地抬起头说。「给俺一口。」

一个倚着榆树的雇工把自己的大杯递给了他。

===Original===
“Ay, ay; thur bean’t no such ale as thur used to be;”﻿—after he had taken his fill.

“I say, Gaffer,” said another fellow, a carter, who had left his horses by the drinking-trough﻿—“I say, Gaffer Pistol-legs, how old bist thee?”

“Aw,” said the patriarch, shaking his head, “I be amazin’ old, I be. I be vourscore and five year come Christmas.”

“Warn you minds a main deal?”

“I minds when the new waterwheel wur put in Fisher’s mill.”
===Chinese===
「可不是，可不是；如今的麦酒不比从前喽；」——这是他灌足了之后说的。

「俺说，老爷子，」另一个伙计说——是个车夫，把马撂在水槽边——「俺说，手枪腿老爷子，恁多大岁数啦？」

「啊，」老寿星摇着头说，「俺老得吓人喽，俺。过了圣诞，俺就八十五啦。」

「那恁记性准好得很吧？」

「俺记得费舍尔家磨坊装新水轮那阵子。」

===Original===
“When wur thuck?”

“Aw, about dree-score year ago.”

“Anything else, Gaffer?”

“Eez; I minds when your grandfeyther wur put in the stocks.”

“Ha, ha! and after all your ’sperience, Pistol-legs, what do ee think be the best theng of all?” said Hedges.
===Chinese===
「那是啥时候的事？」

「啊，约莫六十年前。」

「还有别的没，老爷子？」

「有哩；俺还记得恁爷爷上足枷那回事。」

「哈哈！恁活了这一辈子，见识了这许多，手枪腿，恁说说，顶好的东西到底是啥？」赫奇斯说。

===Original===
“Aw,” said the ancient, picking up his sticks, and delivering his philosophy of the summum bonum with intense gusto. “The vinest theng of all be a horn o’ ale and a lardy-cake!”

“S’pose I must be gettin’ on,” said Hedges presently, and stuffing some hay, which had worked out, into his boot again﻿—for he used hay instead of stockings﻿—he got up, and with Ruck walked down the road.

“A pair of skinvlints,” said the wunt-catcher, looking after them. “One night up to West Farm they was settling a dealing job between um. Zo thur wur a fire, for the snow wur on the ground. Ruck he says, ‘A fire be a terrable waste, you. Let’s put he out.’ Zo they doughted the fire, and both got their feet thegither in a zack.”

By a stile the two farmers thus careful of their fuel were gossiping before parting. “Tell ee what,” said Ruck, in a mysterious tone; “this here dark hoss as Val Browne be training for the autumn yent no go. He doan’t know, and it bean’t no good to tell un﻿—these yer quality be so uppish. But thur be a screw loose somewhere; his trainer be a bad un. Doan’t ee put a crownd on un.”

“Aw, to be zure,” said Hedges. “That there Guss Basset will catch it zum ov these yer days. Squire’s kippur says a’ be allus in th’ wood a-poaching.”

Then they parted, and a curious sight it was. First one would go a few paces, and then stop and talk, and presently come back to the stile again. Then both would walk away, and turn when ten yards distant and gossip, till by degrees they met at the stile once more. Not till this process had been repeated at least five times did they finally separate.
===Chinese===
「啊，」老人家拄起双棍，以十足的兴致发表他那至善之学的哲学。「顶好的东西，就是一角杯麦酒加一块板油蛋糕！」

「俺看得动身了，」过一会儿赫奇斯说；他把蹭出来的干草重新塞回靴筒——他不穿袜子，拿草当袜子——站起身，同拉克一道顺着大路走去。

「一对抠死虱子的，」捕鼹人望着他们的背影说。「有天夜里在西农场（West Farm），他俩正谈一桩买卖。地上积着雪，屋里便生了火。拉克说：『生火可是天大的糟蹋，你。咱把它熄了吧。』他俩就把火弄灭了，两人的脚一并塞进一条麻袋里。」

在一处栅栏梯旁，这两位如此爱惜柴火的农夫临别又攀谈起来。「告诉恁，」拉克神秘兮兮地说，「瓦尔·布朗秋天要训的那匹黑马，成不了事。他自己不知道，告诉他也白搭——这帮上等人架子大着哩。可这里头总有地方不对劲；他那驯马师不是好货。恁可别在他身上押一个克朗。」

「啊，可不，」赫奇斯说。「那个古斯·巴塞特（Guss Basset），早晚有他好受的。乡绅家的看守说，他老是泡在林子里偷猎。」

随后两人分手，那场面煞是有趣。先是一个走出几步，停下说上几句，不久又踱回梯子边。然后两人各自走开，走出十码又都回头搭话，渐渐又凑到栅栏梯跟前。这套程式至少重演了五回，他们才算真正分开。

===Original===
Later in the afternoon Geoffrey strolled out from Thorpe Hall into the park, and sat down under the shade of a huge beech-tree, on the verge of the wood, whence he could just see the roof of Greene Ferne in the meadows far below. There he reclined and pondered﻿—“Where thy treasure is, there will thy heart be also.”

*Tu-whit﻿—tu-what!* came the sound of a scythe being sharpened in a mead below.

Presently he became aware of a heavy footstep approaching; the massive trunk of the beech hid him from sight. It was a milker going to the pen. Geoffrey heard him turn his bucket bottom upwards and sit down upon it.

“Danged ef it bean’t vour, I knaws,” he muttered. “The sun’s over Kingsbury steeple. Wurs Rause (Rose): bean’t hur a-coming?”

Then he began to sing, as milkers do to their cows.
===Chinese===
午后晚些时候，杰弗里从索普庄（Thorpe Hall）踱进园子，在林边一棵大山毛榉的荫下坐了，从那里刚好望得见下方远处草场上格林费恩的屋顶。他斜倚着，反复思量——「你的财宝在哪里，你的心也在哪里。」

*噝啦——噝啦！*山下草场里传来磨镰刀的声音。

不多时，他觉察到一阵沉重的脚步声走近；山毛榉粗大的树干把他遮得严实。是个挤奶的去牛栏。杰弗里听见他把奶桶倒扣过来，坐在了上面。

「俺数得一清二楚，是四只，准是的，」他咕哝道。「日头过了金斯伯里的教堂尖顶了。劳斯（Rause，即萝丝）呢：她还不来？」

接着他唱了起来，挤奶的都这么对着牛唱。

===Original===
“Thee’s got a voice like a wood-pigeon,” said a woman whom Geoffrey heard get over the gate at the corner of the wood. “Thee mumbles, Tummas, like a dumble-dore in a pitcher!”

Geoffrey peeped round the tree, and saw a stout girl in short petticoats, corduroy gaiters, brown hair, and dark eyes.

*Tummas*: Doan’t thee say nought: I hearn thee in church like a charm o’ starlings.

*Rause*: Thee go on to milking.

*Tummas*: I wunt. Come and zet on my knee.

*Rause*: I’ll zee thee in the pond vust with thee gurt vetlocks uppermost.
===Chinese===
「恁这嗓子，活脱一只林鸽，」一个女人的声音说——杰弗里听见她从林子角上的栅门翻了过来。「恁那哼哼，图马斯（Tummas，即托马斯），活像瓮里的大熊蜂！」

杰弗里从树后探头一望，看见一个结实的姑娘，短短的裙子，灯芯绒绑腿，棕色头发，黑眼睛。

*图马斯*：恁少贫嘴：俺在教堂里听恁唱过，活像一窝椋鸟念咒。

*劳斯*：恁挤恁的奶去。

*图马斯*：俺不。过来坐俺腿上。

*劳斯*：俺先瞧着恁栽进池塘里，恁那大脚丫子朝天。

===Original===
*Tummas*: Aw, wooll ee?

*Rause*: Eez, ee wooll.

*Tummas*: Bist a-goin to haymaking to year?

*Rause*: Eez, in the Voremeads to-morrer.

*Tummas*: Zum on um means to gie out and axe for a crownd more. Gwain to strike, doan’t ee zee?

*Rause*: A passel o’ fools.
===Chinese===
*图马斯*：啊，恁敢？

*劳斯*：敢，咋不敢。

*图马斯*：恁今年去晒草不？

*劳斯*：去，明儿在前草甸（Voremeads）。

*图马斯*：有人要撂挑子，要求多加一个克朗。要罢工了，恁懂不？

*劳斯*：一帮子傻子。

===Original===
*Tummas*: Arl on um ull join. They be going to begin at Mrs. Estcourt’s vust down to Greene Ferne. Her be sure to gie in to um, cos her’s a ’ooman.

*Rause*: Odd drot um!

*Tummas*: I zay, Rause.

*Rause*: Eez, you.

*Tummas*: When be we a-goin to do it?

*Rause*: What dost mean?
===Chinese===
*图马斯*：他们全都要入伙。打算先从格林费恩的埃斯特科特太太那儿开场。她准会让着他们，谁叫她是个娘们哩。

*劳斯*：叫他们遭瘟去吧！

*图马斯*：俺说，劳斯。

*劳斯*：哎，恁说。

*图马斯*：咱俩的事儿，啥时候去办？

*劳斯*：恁指啥？

===Original===
*Tummas*: Up to church.

*Rause*: Thee axe Bob vust﻿—he’ll mash thee.

*Tummas*: I’ll warm his jacket ef he puts a vinger on ee. Let’s go up to paason.

*Rause*: Get on with thee.

Geoffrey heard a sound of struggling and two or three resonant kisses.

*Tummas*: Wooll ee come?
===Chinese===
*图马斯*：上教堂呀。

*劳斯*：恁先去问鲍勃（Bob）——他不捣烂恁才怪。

*图马斯*：他敢碰恁一根指头，俺就剥他的皮。咱这就去找牧师。

*劳斯*：去恁的。

杰弗里听见一阵挣扎的动静，还有两三声响亮的亲吻。

*图马斯*：恁到底来不来？

===Original===
*Rause*: Go on whoam with thee.

*Tummas*: Danged ef I’ll stand it! I wunt axe thee no more! Look ee here!

*Rause*: What’s want?

*Tummas*: Woot, or wootn’t?

Off went Rause at a run, and Tummas clattering after. Thought the listener, “Was ever the important question put in straighter terms? Woot, or wootn’t? Will you, or won’t you?”

*Tu-whit﻿—tu-what!* Steadily the scythe was swung, and the swathe fell in rows behind it.
===Chinese===
*劳斯*：恁自个儿家去。

*图马斯*：俺不受这个了！再不问恁了！恁给俺听着！

*劳斯*：要干啥？

*图马斯*：肯，还是不肯？

劳斯撒腿就跑，图马斯踢踢踏踏追去。听的人心想：「那重大的问题，可曾有过比这更直截了当的问法？肯，还是不肯？你愿意，还是不愿意？」

*噝啦——噝啦！*镰刀稳稳地挥动，割下的草条一排排倒落在它身后。

## IV The Wooden Bottle / IV 木酒桶

===Original===
“Gee, Diamond! Now, Captain!” cried Margaret, imitating the gruff voice of the carter. *Crack!* The long-knotted lash of the wagon-whip, bound about the handle with brazen rings, whistled in the air and curled up with a vicious snap. She was in one of her wild impulsive moods. Away trotted the two huge carthorses, the harness merrily jingling and the wagon jolting. Jabez the shepherd could hardly keep pace with it, running beside the leader, Diamond. Margaret and May were riding. *Crack! Crack!*

“Aw, doan’t ’ee now﻿—doan’t ’ee, miss!” panted the shepherd. “Us ull go right drough th’ winder! Whoa!”

For they were steering straight for the great window at Greene Ferne that opened on the lawn. It was wide open that beautiful midsummer morning.

“What are those children doing?” said Mrs. Estcourt, in some alarm. “Why, they have harnessed the horses!”

Valentine, Geoffrey, and Felix, who were there, crowded to the window.
===Chinese===
「驾，『钻石』（Diamond）！喂，『上尉』（Captain）！」玛格丽特（Margaret）喊道，学着车夫的粗嗓门。*啪！*货车长鞭那打满结子的鞭梢——鞭柄上缠着一圈圈黄铜环——在空中呼啸而过，狠狠一甩，卷成一个凶巴巴的鞭花。她正处在她那股子任性的狂兴头上。两匹拉车的大马撒开小跑，挽具叮当欢响，货车颠颠簸簸。牧羊人杰贝兹（Jabez）在领头的「钻石」旁边跟着跑，险些追不上。玛格丽特和梅（May）坐在车上。*啪！啪！*

「啊，小姐，别再甩啦——别再甩啦！」牧羊人直喘。「咱这就要一头撞穿那大窗户啦！吁——！」

因为他们径直朝着格林费恩（Greene Ferne）那扇开向草坪的大窗户冲去。那个晴美的仲夏清晨，窗户正大敞着。

「那两个孩子在干什么？」埃斯特科特太太（Mrs. Estcourt）说，有些惊慌。「哎呀，她们把马套上车了！」

在屋里的瓦伦丁（Valentine）、杰弗里（Geoffrey）和菲利克斯（Felix）一齐涌到窗前。

===Original===
“Whoa, Diamond! Captain, whoa!” cried Margaret, bringing up her convoy on the lawn in fine style. “Now, mamma dear, jump up! We’re all going haymaking, as the men won’t.”

“She has solved the problem,” said Valentine. “Here’s one volunteer!” And he sprang up.

That very morning they had been holding a council to see if anything could be suggested to put an end to the strike. It had now lasted nearly a fortnight. Felix in vain argued with the men; they listened respectfully, and even admitted that he was right; but all they would say was that “they meaned to have th’ crownd.”

Slow to take action, there is no one so stubborn, when once he has resolved, as the agricultural labourer. They had timed the strike with some cunning. They had let the mowers cut some sixty acres of grass, and then suddenly stopped work. They knew very well that if cut grass is allowed to remain exposed to the sun longer than just sufficient to make it into hay, it dries up so much as to be of little value. Now the burning brilliant summer sunshine had been pouring down upon the withered grass for days and days, expelling every particle of the succulent sap, and turning it to a brittle straw. The shepherd, Jabez, remained at his work, and he and Augustus Basset, the “bailie,” did a little, throwing up a mead or two into “wakes” for carting; but their exertions were of small avail. While they talked and deliberated, Margaret, beckoning May, slipped out and went down to the stables. Diamond and Captain were led out into the sunshine, and stood like statuary, waiting to be bidden. For the beauty of simple strength nothing equals a fine carthorse: the vast frame, the ponderous limb, the massive neck, speak of power in repose. Their large dark lustrous eyes followed the girls with calm astonishment; but, docile and gentle, they gave implicit obedience to orders. The heavy harness, brass-mounted, was as much as ever the two girls could manage to lift; and when it came to hoisting up the shafts of the wagon they were at a loss. But Jabez, hearing a noise at the stables, came up; and after him slouched Augustus, muttering to himself, as usual. So just when the council indoors was beginning to wonder what had become of Margaret and May, *crack*, *crack*, and the jingling of harness put an end to their deliberations.

Geoffrey quickly followed Valentine; Felix, more thoughtful, brought a chair for Mrs. Estcourt, who, half laughing, half protesting, against Margaret’s wilful fancy, got up. Augustus sat on the shafts, Jabez stood by the leader, and, seeing them all in, started for the field.
===Chinese===
「吁，钻石！上尉，吁！」玛格丽特喊道，漂漂亮亮地把她的车队停在了草坪上。「来呀，亲爱的妈妈，快上车！既然雇工们不肯干，咱们全家去晒草。」

「她把难题解决了，」瓦伦丁说。「这就来了一个志愿兵！」他一纵身跳上了车。

就在那天早晨，他们还开着会，看能不能想出个法子结束这场罢工。到那时罢工已持续了将近两个礼拜。菲利克斯同雇工们讲道理，白费口舌；他们恭恭敬敬地听，甚至承认他说得对；可他们翻来覆去只有一句：「俺们就是要那一个克朗。」

行动迟缓的农业雇工，一旦拿定主意，天下再没有比他更倔的人。他们把罢工的时机挑得颇为狡黠。他们让割草的人割倒了约莫六十英亩草，然后突然撂了工。他们心里明白得很：割倒的草若任凭烈日暴晒、超过了恰好晒成干草的时辰，就会干得只剩一把枯秸，几乎不值一文。而灼亮耀眼的盛夏骄阳，已经一天又一天地泼在萎蔫的草上，把每一滴浆汁都蒸发殆尽，把它晒成了脆秸。牧羊人杰贝兹没有丢下自己的活计，他同「管事」（bailie）奥古斯塔斯·巴塞特（Augustus Basset）干了点儿——把一两块草场拢成「草垄」（wakes）好装车；可他们那点气力杯水车薪。屋里的人还在议论纷纷，玛格丽特已朝梅一招手，溜出门下了马厩。「钻石」和「上尉」被牵到太阳地里，像两尊雕像立着，只等吩咐。论质朴之力量的美，什么也比不上一匹好挽马：那庞大的骨架、粗重的四肢、厚实的颈项，说的全是蓄而未发的伟力。它们又大又黑、水灵灵的眼睛，带着平静的诧异追着两位姑娘看；但它们温驯柔和，吩咐什么都绝对服从。那套镶着黄铜的重挽具，两个姑娘抬起来已是竭尽全力；到了要抬起货车车辕，她们更是没了办法。杰贝兹听得马厩那头有动静，赶了上来；跟在他后面蹭来的是奥古斯塔斯，照例自顾自嘟哝。于是，正当屋里的会议开始纳闷玛格丽特和梅去了哪儿的时候，*啪*，*啪*，几声鞭响和一阵挽具的叮当，给他们的商议画上了句号。

杰弗里紧跟着瓦伦丁上了车；菲利克斯想得更周到，给埃斯特科特太太搬来一把椅子；她半笑着、半嗔着玛格丽特的任性胡闹，也上了车。奥古斯塔斯坐在车辕上，杰贝兹站在领马旁边，看清人人坐稳，便向地里出发。

===Original===
“If you were to bring out a thirty-six-gallon cask,” said Augustus, whose red nose peered over the front part of the wagon, “and set it up on a haycock, I’ll warn them chaps would come back fast enough.” This was one word for the haymakers and two for himself.

“I’m sure I don’t grudge them some ale,” said Mrs. Estcourt. “But they are really very unreasonable. One of the servants took a mower a quart of beer. He said he did not like it, and didn’t want so much, and poured it out on the grass. Next day only a pint was sent to him: ‘Why y’ent you brought me a quart?’ said he. ‘Because you flung it away,’ was the reply. ‘Aw, that don’t matter. You bring I a quart. I’ll have my mishure,’ (measure); nor should I mind paying the extra five shillings; but you see, Felix, if I pay it, all the farmers round﻿—for they have only struck work on my place, thinking, no doubt, that, being a woman, I must give way﻿—will be obliged to do so, and some of them are not able. Many have called and begged me not; and Mr. Thorpe says the same. Yet I don’t like it. We have always been on good terms with the men.”

“O yes, you pets ’em up,” said Augustus, “just like so many children; and, of course, they ain’t going to work for you.”

“The struggle of capital and labour,” began Felix learnedly, when a sudden jolt of the springless wagon threw him off his balance, and he had to cling to the sides.

“O, mind the gatepost!” cried Mrs. Estcourt, in some alarm.
===Chinese===
「要是搬出一桶三十六加仑的酒来，」奥古斯塔斯说——他那红鼻头从车前板上方探出来——「往草垛上一架，俺敢担保那帮家伙立马就滚回来。」这话一半说给晒草的听，一半说给他自己。

「说真的，给他们喝点麦酒我并不心疼，」埃斯特科特太太说。「可他们实在蛮不讲理。有个仆人给一个割草的送去一夸脱啤酒。他说他不爱喝，用不着这么多，把它泼在了草地上。第二天只给他送了一品脱：『咋不给俺送一夸脱？』他说。『因为上回你泼掉了，』人家回他。『啊，那有啥。你就给俺拿一夸脱来。俺要俺的分量』（measure）；多出那五先令我也不介意掏；可是您瞧，菲利克斯，我要是付了，周围所有的农场主——因为雇工们只在我这儿罢工，无疑是琢磨着我是妇道人家，必定让步——就都得跟着付，有几户人家付不起。好些人登门求我别松口；索普先生（Mr. Thorpe）也这么说。可我心里不痛快。我们同雇工们向来处得不错。」

「可不，您把他们都宠坏了，」奥古斯塔斯说，「当一群娃娃惯着；那他们自然不肯给您干活喽。」

「资本与劳动之争——」菲利克斯学究气地开了腔，不防没有弹簧的货车猛一颠，把他掀得东倒西歪，赶紧抓住了车帮。

「哎，当心门柱！」埃斯特科特太太喊道，有点着慌。

===Original===
While they were near the house Jabez went slow; but the moment he reached the open field away he started, and what with the jingling of the harness, the creaking of the wheels, and the necessity for holding on tight, conversation became impossible. The wagon rose up and sloped down over the furrows of the meadow as a boat pitches in a sea.

“Woaght! whoa!” shouted Jabez, drawing up among the hay. “This be it; the prongs be in the ditch.”

When they had descended, he went to the hawthorn bush, pulled out some prongs, and then scrambled up into the wagon himself. “Now then, you lards and gennelmen, one on ’ee get each side, and pitch up thaay wakes (ridges of hay put ready for the purpose of loading), and mind as you doan’t stick your farks into I. The wimmen﻿—I means the ladies﻿—wull rake behind, and paason can help um﻿—th’ rakes be hung on th’ hedge. Now, bailie, look arter them ’osses.”

Though hay looks light and easy to lift, yet when the fork has gathered a goodly bundle, to hoist it high overhead, and continue the operation, is really heavy labour. Valentine was physically a smaller made man than Geoffrey, whose broad shoulders had also been developed both by athletic exercise at home and by work in Australia﻿—work done from choice, not necessity. But though smaller, Valentine was extremely tough, wiry, and nimble, as is often the case with gentlemen who “fancy” horses. Quick in his movements, he caught the knack of “pitching” almost immediately. He hastily flung up his “wake” as far as the horse in the shafts, and then walked to the rear of the wagon where Margaret was raking, leaving Geoffrey still engaged.

Margaret and May were looking at a nest of harvest-trows, as the tiny mice are called that breed in the grass. Valentine began to talk about his horses, knowing Margaret was fond of animals, and said that a “string” of his would pass Greene Ferne in the evening en route to his stables. Now Geoffrey, glancing back, saw the group apparently in earnest conversation from which he was excluded; and noting Margaret’s attention to Valentine, grew jealous and angry. Just as he finished “pitching,” and was about to join them﻿—
===Chinese===
车子还在宅子近旁时，杰贝兹走得慢；一上了开阔的田地，他立刻撒了欢——挽具叮当、车轮吱嘎，再加上必须抓紧坐稳，说话都成了不可能。货车在草场的犁垄上一起一伏，像小船在海里颠簸。

「吁——！吁！」杰贝兹喊道，把车停在干草中间。「就是这儿；草叉撂在水沟里。」

众人下了车，他走到山楂丛那儿，抽出几把草叉，自己手脚并用地爬上了货车。「好了，恁们这些老爷们、绅士们，一边站一个，把那些草垄（预先拢好待装的干草垄）叉上车来，可当心别把叉子戳到俺身上。娘们——俺是说太太小姐们——在后头搂耙，牧师可以搭把手——耙子都挂在树篱上。喂，管事的，看好那几匹马。」

干草看着轻巧好举，可草叉一旦叉起满满一大捆，要把它高高举过头顶扬上车去，还要一连串干下去，可实在是个重活。论体格，瓦伦丁比杰弗里单薄些；杰弗里那副宽肩膀，又是靠在家时的体育锻炼、又是靠在澳大利亚的劳作——出于自愿而非迫于生计——练出来的。但瓦伦丁虽然瘦小，却极其坚韧、精悍、机敏，「痴迷」马匹的绅士们往往如此。他动作快，几乎立刻就摸到了「叉草上车」的窍门。他急急忙忙把自己那侧的草垄一路叉到车辕马跟前，然后走到货车后部玛格丽特搂草的地方，把杰弗里撇在那儿还没干完。

玛格丽特和梅正在看一窝「收割鼠」（harvest-trows）——人们这么称呼在草里做窝繁殖的那种小老鼠。瓦伦丁谈起他的马来，他知道玛格丽特喜欢牲口，还说傍晚他有一「串」马要路过格林费恩，回他的马厩去。杰弗里回头一瞥，正看见那一伙人谈得起劲，唯独把他撇在一旁；他见玛格丽特那般专注地听瓦伦丁说话，妒火和怒气便腾了起来。他刚把自己那侧的草叉完，正要凑过去——

===Original===
“Tchek!” from Augustus, and on the horses moved, and he had to recommence work. Valentine ran with his prong, and again, by dint of great exertions, finished his side first, and returned to Margaret.

“Tchek! woaght!”

The third time Valentine essayed the same task, delighted to leave Geoffrey in the cold, and to exhibit his superior prowess. But Geoffrey by now had learned how to handle his fork. His muscles were strung, his blood was up, he warmed to his work, and pitched vast bundles that all but buried and half choked Jabez, who was loading on the wagon.

“The dust be all down my droat! Aw, doan’t ’ee, measter!” he cried, in smothered tones.

“Tchek!” and this time Valentine was far behind, and Geoffrey had gone back to talk to Margaret. At the next move Geoffrey not only cleared his side up to the horse in the shafts, but by using his great strength to the utmost, went ahead up the wake eight or ten yards, and thus secured himself twice as long with her, while Valentine had to remain “pitching.” To Jabez the shepherd, on the wagon, it was fine sport to watch the rivalry of the “gennelmen.” A labouring man thoroughly enjoys seeing the perspiration pouring from the faces of the well-to-do. He bustled about as fast as he could, and kept the horses moving. By superior muscular force Geoffrey remained ahead. To Valentine it was gall and wormwood.
===Chinese===
「嘚儿！」奥古斯塔斯一声吆喝，马又朝前挪，他只得从头再干。瓦伦丁提着草叉一路小跑，又是靠一番拼命使劲，先干完了自己那侧，回到玛格丽特身边。

「嘚儿！吁——！」

第三回，瓦伦丁又打算如法炮制，得意于把杰弗里晾在一边，好显摆自己高人一筹的身手。可这时的杰弗里已经摸熟了草叉的脾气。他筋肉绷紧，热血上涌，越干越起劲，叉起的一大捆一大捆草几乎把车上装草的杰贝兹埋了起来，呛得他半死。

「草灰全灌进俺嗓子眼儿啦！啊，别叉啦，老爷！」他憋着嗓子喊。

「嘚儿！」这一回瓦伦丁被远远甩在了后头，杰弗里已经回去同玛格丽特说话了。车再往前挪时，杰弗里不但把自己那侧清到了车辕马跟前，还使出浑身的蛮力，顺着草垄超前叉出了八九码十来码，如此便挣得加倍的光阴陪在她身边，而瓦伦丁只好留在原地「叉草」。对车上装草的牧羊人杰贝兹来说，看着两位「绅士」争风吃醋，真是好戏。做工的人最爱看有钱人汗珠子滚滚淌。他脚下加紧忙活，一个劲儿让马往前挪。杰弗里仗着膂力过人，始终占先。对瓦伦丁，这可是苦胆和茵陈。

===Original===
“We be getting on famous, zur,” said Jabez. “Tchek!”

Mrs. Estcourt had meantime left the field, after beckoning to Augustus, who followed her. While she was present there was some check on their rivalry; but no sooner did they perceive that she was gone than it rose to a still greater height. Valentine, pulling himself together, and taking advantage of a thinner wake than usual, ran ahead, and went back to the rear. Seeing this, Geoffrey hurled the hay up with such force and vigour that he literally covered the shepherd, who could barely struggle out of it.

“Lord, I be as dry as a gicks!” said Jabez, when he did get free, and meaning by his simile the stem of a dead hedge-plant.

“And here’s bailie wi’ th’ bottle. Bide a bit, my lards.”

By this time “my lards” thoroughly understood why haymakers like their ale, and plenty of it. Working under the hot sun, with the dust or dry pollen flying from the hay, causes intense thirst. So the wagon stood still, and Valentine, hot and angry, took the bottle﻿—being the nearest﻿—from Augustus, and essayed to drink. This “bottle” was a miniature cask of oaken staves, with iron hoops, and a leathern strap to carry it by. It held about a gallon. To drink, the method is to put the lips to the bunghole, situate at the largest part of the circumference, toss the barrel up, and hold the head back. Valentine could not get more than the merest sip, though the bottle was quite full. This, scientifically speaking, was caused by the pressure of the atmosphere. There is the same difficulty in drinking from a flask.
===Chinese===
「咱们干得可真叫漂亮，长官，」杰贝兹说。「嘚儿！」

这当儿埃斯特科特太太已朝奥古斯塔斯招了招手，离了草场，奥古斯塔斯跟了去。她在场时，两人的较量还有个节制；可他们一发觉她走了，较量顿时又攀上新高。瓦伦丁重整旗鼓，趁着一垄比往常薄的草，抢先赶到了前头，又折回车尾。杰弗里看在眼里，抡起草来又猛又狠，简直是连人带草把牧羊人整个埋了，他好半天才从草里挣扎出来。

「老天，俺干得跟根枯秆儿似的！」杰贝兹总算脱身出来——他这比喻，说的是枯死的树篱苗子的秆儿。

「喏，管事的拎桶来啦。稍待一会儿，老爷们。」

到这时，「老爷们」算是彻底明白了，晒草的人为什么个个爱麦酒，还要喝个够。大太阳底下干活，草屑和干花粉扑面飞扬，渴得人嗓子冒烟。于是货车停了下来；又热又气的瓦伦丁——他离得最近——从奥古斯塔斯手里接过「桶」，试着喝。这「桶」是橡木板条拼的小桶，箍着铁箍，带一根提梁皮带。容量约一加仑。喝法是：把嘴凑在桶孔上——孔开在桶肚最粗处——仰起桶，头往后仰。可瓦伦丁灌了半天，只抿着了那么一小口，虽然桶是满的。从科学上讲，这是大气压力所致。用扁瓶喝水，也有同样的难处。

===Original===
“Let th’ aair in﻿—let th’ aair in!” said the shepherd, himself an adept. “Open th’ carner of yer mouth.”

But attempting to do that Val let too much “aair” in, and spilt the ale, to his intense disgust.

“Put th’ cark in, zur, and chuck un up to I.” Jabez caught the “bottle” as tenderly as a mother would her infant and quitted not his hold till half the contents had disappeared, nor would he have left it then, had not Augustus grumbled and claimed his turn. Mrs. Estcourt now returned, attended by a servant carrying a basket of refreshments for which she had gone, not forgetting the more civilised bottles issued by the divine Bass. Throwing down forks and rakes, they assembled in the shade of the tall hawthorn hedge and sat down on the hay. When the delicate flavour of his cigar floated away on the soft summer air, even Valentine’s acerbity of temper relaxed. Opposite, at some distance, stood the wagon now fully loaded; Diamond and Captain eating the hay put for them, and the shepherd lying at full length on the grass. Augustus, the “bottle” by his side, and his hand laid lovingly on it, fell asleep in the shade of the wagon.

The wild-roses on the briars that stretched out from the hedge towards the meadow opened their petals full to the warmth. The breeze rustled the leaves of the elm overhead. Rich flute-like notes of music came from the copse hard by﻿—it was the blackbird.

“Ah, this is merry England,” said Felix, who loved his cigar, watching the tiny cloud float away from its tip. “The blackbird sings in the scorching sun at noonday, when the other songbirds are silent. You did not know Geof was a writer, did you?” He drew forth a piece of paper, when Newton began to protest, and would have taken it from him by main force, had not the ladies insisted on hearing the contents. So Felix read the verses.

> **Noontide in the Meadow.**
===Chinese===
「放气进去——放气进去！」牧羊人喊，他本人是个行家。「把嘴角张开条缝儿。」

可瓦尔（Val）一试，「气」放进了太多，麦酒泼了出来，气得他直恶心。

「把桶塞塞上，长官，扔给俺。」杰贝兹接住那「桶」，像母亲接婴孩一样温柔，不喝掉半桶绝不松手；若不是奥古斯塔斯嘟哝着轮到他了，他还不肯罢休。埃斯特科特太太回来了，身后跟着个仆人，提着她去取的一篮子茶点——也没忘记那更为文明的瓶装货，神品巴斯（Bass）。众人撂下草叉和耙子，聚到高大山楂篱的荫凉里，在干草上坐了下来。雪茄的清芬在夏日和风里袅袅飘散，连瓦伦丁的火气也消了。对面稍远处停着装满了的货车；「钻石」和「上尉」嚼着为它们摊下的草，牧羊人四仰八叉躺在草地上。奥古斯塔斯，那「桶」搁在身边，一只手亲亲热热地搭在上面，在车影里睡着了。

从树篱伸向草场的野蔷薇，朝着暖意把花瓣开足了。微风沙沙吹动头顶榆树的叶子。矮林那边飘来笛子般丰润的乐音——是乌鸫。

「啊，这就是快活的英格兰，」菲利克斯说——他爱他的雪茄，望着烟尖上飘起的小小烟云。「乌鸫在晌午灼人的太阳底下唱歌，别的鸣禽都哑了的时候。你们不知道杰夫（Geof）会写诗吧？」他抽出一张纸；牛顿连声抗议，若不是太太小姐们执意要听，他简直要动武抢下来了。于是菲利克斯念了那几节诗。

> **草场正午。**

===Original===
> Idly silent were the finches﻿—
 

 Finches fickle, fleeting, blithe;
 

 And the mower, man of inches,
 

 Ceased to swing the sturdy scythe.

> All the leafy oaks were slumb’rous;
 

 Slumb’rous e’en the honeybee;
 
===Chinese===
> 雀鸟懒懒，悄无声息——

　雀鸟轻浮、易逝、欢快；

　割草的汉子身量魁伟，

　也停了挥动那结实的镰。

　多叶的橡树睡意沉沉；

　蜜蜂也睡意沉沉；

===Original===
 And his larger brother, cumbrous,
 

 Humming home with golden knee.

> But the blackbird, king of hedgerows﻿—
 

 Hedgerows to my memory dear﻿—
 

 By the brook, where rush and sedge grows,
 

 Sang his liquid love-notes clear.
===Chinese===
　它那更大的兄弟行动笨拙，

　带着金膝嗡嗡归巢。

　可乌鸫，树篱之王——

　树篱，我记忆里的亲爱——

　在灯心草与薹草丛生的溪畔，

　唱出它清亮的情歌。

===Original===
Margaret, toying with a June rose﻿—the white petal delicately tinted with pink between her soft rosy fingers﻿—dreamily repeated half to herself﻿—

> “All the leafy oaks were slumb’rous.”

Valentine glanced at her swiftly, and inwardly resolved to remove the impression on her mind. He took out his pocketbook.

“My verses,” said he, “are only copied, but they seemed to me a gem in their way. It is a piece of Bacchic meditation from the Vaux de Vire, exquisitely translated by some clever author whose name I have forgotten. You are gazing at our friend Augustus’ bibulous nose,” he nodded towards the recumbent figure with the hand on the bottle, “and see it through your own glass:

> “Fair nose! whose beauties many pipes have cost
 

 Of white and rosy wine;
 
===Chinese===
玛格丽特把玩着一朵六月玫瑰——粉晕轻染的白花瓣夹在她柔软的玫红指间——半自语地、恍恍惚惚地念着——

> 「多叶的橡树睡意沉沉。」

瓦伦丁飞快地瞥了她一眼，心里暗暗打定主意，要抹去她心里这个印象。他掏出了记事本。

「我的诗，」他说，「只是抄来的，可在我看来，也算得它那一类里的珍品。这是《维尔河谷歌谣》（Vaux de Vire）里的一首酒神冥想曲，被哪位高明的作者译得精妙之至，名字我忘了。你正瞧着咱们朋友奥古斯塔斯那只嗜酒的鼻子，」他朝那个手搭酒桶躺着的人影点点头，「而且是透过你自己的杯子瞧的：

> 「美鼻啊！你的美貌令多少杯

　白葡萄酒玫瑰酒付诸东流；

===Original===
 Whose colours are so gorgeously embossed
 

 In red and purple fine;
 

 Great nose, who views thee, gazing through great glass,
 

 Thee still more lovely thinks.
 

 Thou dost the nose of creature far surpass
 

 Who only water drinks.”
===Chinese===
　你的色泽如此华彩凸雕，

　在红紫的上色里精工细描；

　伟大的鼻子，谁若隔着重杯端详你，

　只会觉得你更加可爱。

　你远远胜过一切生灵的鼻子——

　那些只喝白水的家伙。」

===Original===
It was so appropriate to poor Augustus that they could not choose but smile. Valentine begged Margaret to sing: they all joined in the request, and she sang with a faint blush, looking down﻿—for she knew, though the rest did not, that it was Geoffrey’s favourite﻿—the beautiful old ballad of the “Bailiff’s Daughter of Islington.” With the wild-rose in her hand, the delicate bloom on her cheek, the green hedge behind, the green elm above, and the sweet scent of the hay, she looked the ballad as well as sang it.

“Ah,” said Felix, “no sign of study in those old ballads, no premeditation, no word-twisting and jerking; rugged metre so involved that none can understand it without pondering an hour or two. This is the way we criticise poetry nowadays, in our mechanical age﻿—just listen: somebody has been measuring Tennyson with a foot-rule. I read from a professor’s analysis﻿—‘The line is varied by dactylic or iambic substitution, as well as by truncation and anacrusis;’ ‘the line is varied by anapaestic and trochaic (rarely dactylic) substitutions, and by initial truncation.’ As Faust says, not all these word-twisters have ever made a Maker yet.”

*Crash!*﻿—splintering of wood and breaking of boughs.

“Here gwoes! Come on, you! Hoorah! Us ull put it up, missus; doan’t ’ee be afeared! you bin a good missus to we. So into’t, you vellers!”

Eight or ten men came crashing through a gap in the hedge, and seizing the prongs and rakes that were lying about with no more explanation than these brief ejaculations, dashed out to work. Heartily tired of rambling idly about, hands in pockets, seeing no prospect of the men on the other farms joining them, they had been hanging round the place in a sheepish way, till, finally observing the ladies working, the sense of shame got the better, and they made a rush for the hay, and gave up the strike. For there is sterling worth and some rude chivalry in these men, though simple enough, and easily led astray; the more the pity that no one has yet taken the lead among them with a view to their own real and solid advancement.
===Chinese===
这诗用在可怜的奥古斯塔斯身上再贴切不过，众人不由得不笑。瓦伦丁请玛格丽特唱一支：大家都跟着央求，她微微红着脸、垂着眼唱了——因为她知道（旁人并不知道），那是杰弗里最心爱的一支——美丽的古歌谣《伊斯灵顿的管事之女》（The Bailiff's Daughter of Islington）。她手里握着野蔷薇，颊上晕着娇红，身后是绿篱，头顶是绿榆，空气中浮着干草的甜香——她把这支歌谣唱了出来，也把这支歌谣活活演了出来。

「啊，」菲利克斯说，「那些古歌谣里没有苦学的痕迹，没有预谋，没有字句的拧绞与牵扯；粗粝的音律盘根错节，不琢磨上一两个钟头谁也闹不懂。如今我们就是这么批评诗歌的，在我们这机械时代——听听：有人拿直尺量过丁尼生（Tennyson）。我念一段教授的分析——『诗行以扬抑抑格或抑扬格的替换加以变化，亦借截短与起音错位；』『诗行以抑抑扬格与扬抑格（偶见扬抑抑格）的替换加以变化，并借句首截短。』正如《浮士德》（Faust）所说，这伙拧字眼的，还从来没能拧出一位诗人来。」

*哗啦！*——木头的断裂，树枝的折响。

「这就干！来呀，伙计们！好哇！俺们把它装上车，太太；恁别害怕！恁待俺们一直是好太太。使劲干吧，伙计们！」

八个十个月人从树篱的一个豁口里噼里啪啦冲了出来，抓起四处撂着的草叉和耙子，除了这几声呐喊再没有半句解释，便一鼓作气干起活来。他们本来就逛荡得两手插兜、实在腻烦，眼见别的庄园的伙计们没有响应的意思，一直讪讪地在附近打转；到后来一见太太小姐们亲自干活，羞耻心终于占了上风，便朝干草冲了过去，把罢工丢到了脑后。这些人身上自有真金般的品质，还有些粗豪的侠气，尽管头脑简单，容易被人带偏；可惜至今没有人领头，为他们的真实而坚实的长进出力——这正是可叹之处。

===Original===
“I will go home and send them some refreshment,” said Mrs. Estcourt. All the party rose and accompanied her. In the next field they passed the mowers preparing to begin mowing again. Geoffrey and Valentine both tried to mow, but utterly failed; the point of the scythe persistently stuck into the ground.

“A’ be a’ akkerd tool for a body as bean’t used to un,” said the eldest of the men, taking out his stone rubber from the sling at his back, preparatory to giving the scythe a touch up after such rough handling; “and um bean’t what um used to be when I wur a bwoy.”

“How do you mean?” said Felix.

“Aw,” said the mower, tilting his hat back, “th’ blades be as good as ever um wur﻿—thaay folk at Mells be th’ vellers to make scythes. Thur bean’t none as good as thaim. But it be th’ handle, look’ee, as I means. I minds when thaay wur made of dree sarts of wood, a main bit more crooked than this yer stick, and sart o’ carved a bit; doant ’ee see? It took a chap a week zumtimes to find a bit a’ wood as ud do. But, bless ee, a’moast anything does now.”

*Swish* went the keen blade through the tall grass. They watched him a few minutes.

“Thur be some blight about,” said the man; “scythe do scum up terrable,” and he showed them the blade all covered with a greenish-white froth, supposed to be caused by insects. “Thur be blight up thur, look.”
===Chinese===
「我这就回家，给他们弄些茶点送去。」埃斯特科特太太说。众人都起身陪她回去。经过邻地时，他们看见割草的人正准备重新开镰。杰弗里和瓦伦丁都试了试割草，全然不成；镰尖一而再再而三地扎进土里。

「使不惯的人使它，它就是件别扭的家什，」那年岁最大的说，从背上的皮套里掏出磨刀石，预备在这番粗使之后把镰刀蹭亮蹭亮；「如今的家什，也不是俺做小伙子那时候的样子喽。」

「这话怎么讲？」菲利克斯说。

「啊，」割草人把帽子往后一推，「刀片还跟从前一样好——梅尔斯（Mells）那帮人就是打镰刀的好手。天下没有比他们更好的。可俺说的是这柄，恁瞧。俺记得从前的柄是用三种木头拼的，比如今这根棍子弯得多，还多少雕了点花；恁说是不是？有时候，为寻一块合适的木料，能花一个小伙子一礼拜的工夫。可如今哩，老天保佑恁，啥玩意儿都能凑合。」

*唰*——锋利的刀刃扫过高草。他们看了他几分钟。

「这草里有虫害，」那人说；「镰刀上积的泡沫腥得厉害，」他把刀刃指给他们看，上面糊满了绿白色的泡沫，据说是虫子闹的。「瞧，上头那片有虫害。」

===Original===
He pointed to a dark heavy cloud that seemed to float at a great height in the east.

“It will thunder,” said May.

“Aw, no it wunt, miss,” said the mower. “A’ reckon as it’ll be nation hot; thuck cloud be nothing but blight. Spile the fruit, bless’ee.”

“So even the scythe handles used to be artistic,” said Felix, as they walked away. “There used to be art and taste and workmanship even in so common a thing. It was made of three distinct pieces of wood, carefully finished off; men took days to find a piece. Now it is nothing but a stick smoothed by machinery. I *hate* machinery. I like to see the artist in his work; to see the mark of the knife where the chip has been taken out. But the spirit of art flies when things are sent forth by machinery﻿—hundreds exactly alike.”

To May it was a great pleasure to hear him dilate in this way. Near the house they met Augustus, radiant with smiles, and perfectly loaded with the wooden bottles for the men.

“I knows I’m a fool,” said he; “at least I ought to, since I’m told so forty times a day. But a fool must be sometimes right. ’Pend upon it, there’s nothing like ale!”
===Chinese===
他指着东方高空里一团沉沉的乌云。

「要打雷了。」梅说。

「啊，打不了，小姐，」割草人说。「俺估摸着，热得才邪乎哩；那团云不是别的，是虫瘴。要毁果子喽，老天保佑恁。」

「这么说，连镰刀柄从前都讲究艺术，」他们走开时菲利克斯说，「就连这么普通的一件东西，从前也有艺术、趣味和手艺在里头。它是用三截不同的木头做的，精心修整收尾；人们肯花几天工夫去找一块料。如今不过是机器磨光的一根棍子。我*恨*机器。我爱看工作中的艺术家；爱看削掉木片处留下的刀痕。可东西一经机器出手——成百上千一模一样——艺术的魂灵就飞走了。」

听他这样高谈阔论，梅只觉得大享其乐。宅子近旁，他们遇见奥古斯塔斯，他满面春风，身上挂满了给雇工们带的木酒桶。

「俺知道自己是个傻瓜，」他说；「至少该知道，一天总有人跟俺说上四十回。可傻瓜也有说对的时候。你放心，啥也比不上麦酒！」

===Original===
At Greene Ferne, May found a letter for her which spoilt the day. It was from her grandfather, Andrew Fisher, of the Warren, written in great anger, and commanding her immediate return home, and to mind and bring that rug with her that had been at Greene Ferne ever since Christmas. The old grasping miser, in his rage, remembered such a trifle as a travelling-rug. Fisher had sent a verbal message for his granddaughter before, which she had ventured to put off; now he wrote in a furious temper, and added at the foot that if that parson ever came anigh the Warren again he’d have him ducked in the mill-pool. So bitter had the mere thought made him that Felix wanted his money. There was nothing for it but for May to return, and she asked for her horse to be saddled. Felix could hardly suppress his annoyance. May was much downcast, but Margaret cheered her.

“I will go with you,” she said. “He was always nice to me. He is a regular old flatterer,”﻿—(she peeped in the glass)﻿—“only think, flattering at ninety! But a man must flatter, if he’s a hundred! I shall get over him! I’ll ride my chestnut, and I can stay with you, dear, can’t I? and come back next evening.”

So they left together. Geoffrey, in shaking hands with Margaret, tried to whisper, “May I come and meet you tomorrow evening?” but could not well manage it, Valentine being near.

“Be sure and return by the road, dear,” said Mrs. Estcourt﻿—“the Downs are very lonely if you come by yourself, and you may lose your way.”

“Oh, no,” laughed Margaret. “I love the hills, and I know them all. I must come over the turf, mamma dear.”

Now, Geoffrey heard this, and mentally noted it. He had his horse at Thorpe Hall, and he determined to ride and meet Margaret on the morrow.
===Chinese===
在格林费恩，梅接到一封写给她的信，把这一天全毁了。信是她祖父、沃伦的安德鲁·费舍尔（Andrew Fisher）写来的，字里行间怒气冲冲，勒令她即刻回家，还叮嘱别忘了把那条毯子捎回来——打圣诞节起它就一直撂在格林费恩。这老贪婪鬼，盛怒之中竟还记得旅行毯这样的小事。先前费舍尔已捎过口信叫孙女回去，她斗胆拖延了；这回他写信来，火冒三丈，还在信尾添了一句：那牧师要是再敢靠近沃伦一步，就叫人把他摁进磨池里浸水。单单想到菲利克斯图他的钱，就叫他恨成这样。梅别无选择，只得回去，她吩咐给她的马备鞍。菲利克斯几乎按捺不住心头的恼怒。梅垂头丧气，倒是玛格丽特给她打气。

「我陪你去，」她说。「他一向待我好。他是个地道的老奉承精，」——（她朝镜子里瞟了一眼）——「想想看，九十岁还爱听奉承！男人嘛，活到一百岁也得奉承！我会把他降住的！我骑我的栗色马，我可以陪你住下，亲爱的，行不？明儿傍晚再回来。」

于是她们一同动身。杰弗里同玛格丽特握手时，想说句悄悄话：「明天傍晚我可以来迎你吗？」可瓦伦丁就在近旁，话不好出口。

「千万走大路回来，亲爱的，」埃斯特科特太太说——「一个人走丘原太冷清了，说不定还会迷路。」

「哎呀，不会的，」玛格丽特笑道。「我爱丘原，哪儿我都认得。我一定要从草坡上回来，亲爱的妈妈。」

这话恰好叫杰弗里听见了，心里暗暗记下。他的马拴在索普庄（Thorpe Hall），他打定主意，明天骑马去迎玛格丽特。

## I “Up to Church.” / I 「上教堂去」

===Original===
“Fine growing marning, you.”

“Ay, casualty weather, though.”

*Ding﻿—ding﻿—dill! Dill﻿—ding﻿—dill!* This last was the cracked bell of the village church ringing “to service.” The speakers were two farmers, who, after exchanging greeting, leant against the churchyard wall, and looked over, as they had done every fine-weather Sunday this thirty years. So regular was this pressure, that the moss which covered the coping-stones elsewhere was absent from the spot where they placed their arms. On the other side of the wall, and on somewhat lower ground, was a pigsty, beyond that a cow-yard, then a barn and some ricks. “Casualty,” used in connection with weather, means uncertain. Mr. Hedges, the taller of the two men, stooped a good deal; he wore a suit of black, topped, however, by a billycock. Mr. Ruck, very big and burly, was shaped something like one of his own mangolds turned upside-down: that is to say, as the glance ran over his figure, beginning at the head, it had to take in a swelling outline as it proceeded lower. He was clad in a snowy-white smock-frock, breeches and gaiters, and glossy beaver hat.

This costume had a hieroglyphic meaning. The showy smock-frock intimated that he had risen from lowly estate, and was proud of the fact. The breeches and gaiters gave him an air of respectable antiquity in itself equivalent to a certain standing. Finally the beaver hat﻿—which everybody in the parish knew cost a guinea, and nothing less﻿—bespoke the thousand pounds at the bank to which he so frequently alluded.

*Dill﻿—ding﻿—ding! Ding﻿—dill﻿—dill!*
===Chinese===
「好个催庄稼的早晨，恁说。」

「是啊，可这天儿没个准头。」

*叮——叮——的！的——叮——的！*这后一种声音，是村子教堂那口哑了嗓的钟在敲「礼拜开始」。说话的是两个农夫，寒暄过后，便像三十年来每个晴朗礼拜天那样，靠着教堂墓园的墙往里张望。这处倚靠如此规律，以至于别处盖满压顶石的青苔，唯独他们搁胳膊的地方寸苔不生。墙外那边、地势略低处是个猪圈，再过去是牛栏，然后是谷仓和几座草垛。「没个准头」（casualty），用来形容天气，意思就是靠不住。两人中高些的那位赫奇斯先生（Mr. Hedges），背驼得厉害；一身黑衣裳，头上却扣了顶圆顶毡帽。拉克先生（Mr. Ruck）生得魁梧粗壮，体形有点像他自家地里的一只饲用甜菜倒了个个儿：也就是说，目光从他头上扫下去，越往下越要收进一圈鼓鼓的轮廓。他穿着雪白的罩衫、马裤和绑腿，头上一顶锃亮的海狸皮帽。

这身行头自有一套象形文字般的含义。那件招摇的罩衫表明他出身微贱，且引以为荣。马裤和绑腿给他添了一种体面的古风，其本身便抵得上相当的身份。至于那顶海狸皮帽——教区里人人知道它值一个几尼，少一个子儿也不行——则昭示着他在银行里的那一千镑，那可是他三天两头挂在嘴边的。

*的——叮——叮！叮——的——叮！*

===Original===
The sweet spring air breathed softly; the warm sunshine fell on the old grey church, whose shadow slowly receded from the tombstones and low grassy mounds. The rounded ridge of the Downs rose high to the south﻿—so near that the fleecy clouds sailing up were not visible till they slid suddenly into view over the summit. Tiny toy-like sheep, reduced in size by the distance were dotted here and there on the broad slope. Over the corn hard by, the larks sprang up and sang at so great a height that the motion of their wings could not be distinguished. The earth exhaled a perfume, there was music in the sky, a caress in the breeze. Far down in the vale a sheet of water glistened; beyond that the forest of trees and hedges became indistinct, and assumed a faint blue tint, extending like the sea, till heaven and earth mingled at the hazy horizon.

*Humph﻿—humph!* The pigs were thrusting their noses into a heap of rubbish piled up against the wall, and covered with docks and nettles. Mr. Hedges leant a little farther over the coping, and with the end of his stick rubbed the back of the fattest, producing divers grunts of satisfaction. This operation seemed to give equal pleasure to the man and the animal.

“Thirteen score,” said Ruck sententiously, referring to the weight of the said pig.

“Mebbe a bit more, you,”﻿—two farmers could by no possibility agree on the weight of an animal. “Folk never used to think nothing of a peg till a’ were nigh on twenty score. But this generation be nice in bacon, and likes a wafer rasher as shrivels up dry without a lick of grease.”

“It be a spectacle to see the chaps in the Lunnon eating-houses pick over their plates,” said Ruck. “Such a waste of good vittels!”
===Chinese===
甜美的春空气轻柔地呼吸；暖煦的阳光落在灰色的老教堂上，教堂的影子从墓碑和低矮的青冢上缓缓退去。丘原浑圆的脊线在南边高高耸起——近得那扬帆而来的朵朵白云，要突然滑过山顶才映入眼帘。小小的、玩具似的绵羊，被距离缩得小小的，星星点点散布在宽阔的山坡上。近旁的麦田上空，云雀直冲上天，唱得那么高，高得连翅膀的扑动都分辨不出。大地吐着芬芳，天空里有音乐，微风里有爱抚。山谷深处一片水光潋滟；再往远，树林与树篱的海洋模糊了，蒙上一层淡蓝，像大海一样铺展开去，直到天地相融于朦胧的地平线。

*哼哧——哼哧！*猪正把鼻子拱进一堆靠墙堆着的垃圾里，垃圾上盖满了酸模和荨麻。赫奇斯先生又朝墙那头探了探身，用手杖头给最肥的那只搔起背来，搔得它发出种种满足的哼哼。这一操作给人给猪带来的快活，似乎不相上下。

「十三个二十磅，」拉克一本正经地说，说的是那头猪的分量。

「兴许还多点儿，恁说，」——一头牲口的分量，两个农夫是万万不可能谈得拢的。「从前的人，一口猪不到二十个二十磅，根本不放在眼里。可如今这代人吃培根讲究，就爱薄得跟纸似的熏肉条，煎起来干缩缩的，一点儿油花都不冒。」

「伦敦馆子里那帮小子挑挑拣拣扒拉盘子里的吃食，那才叫一道风景，」拉克说。「好好的粮食，糟蹋啦！」

===Original===
“There’ll be a judgment on it some day.” The click of the double wicket-gates﻿—double, to keep other people’s sheep out and the rector’s sheep in﻿—now began to sound more frequently, as the congregation gathered by twos and threes, coming up the various footpaths that led across the fields. Very few entered the church﻿—most hanging about and forming little groups as their acquaintances came up. The boys stole away from their gossiping parents, and got together where a projecting buttress and several high square tombs formed a recess and hid their proceedings. A broad sunken slab just there was level with the turf; the grass grew over at the edges. They had scraped away the moss that covered it; the inscription had long since disappeared, except the figure 7, a remnant of the date. Something like the chink of coppers on stone might have been heard now and then, when there was a lull in their chatter.

*Dill﻿—dill!*

“Squire Thorpe got visitors, yent a’?” asked Hedges, perfectly well aware of the fact, but desirous of learning something else, and getting at it sideways, as country folk will.

“Aw; that tall fellow, Geoffrey Newton, and Val Browne, as have set up the training-stables.”

“Warn he’ll want some hay?” This was a leading question, and Hedges rubbed away at the pig to appear innocently unconcerned.
===Chinese===
「早晚有报应。」双扇小门——之所以双扇，是为了把别人的羊挡在外头、把教区长的羊圈在里头——的咔嗒声，此刻响得勤了；会众三三两两，顺着横穿田野的条条小径聚拢来。进教堂的寥寥无几——大多数人在外头转悠，熟人来了便扎成一小堆一小堆。孩子们从闲磕牙的爹娘身边溜走，聚到一个凸出的扶壁和几座高方墓围成的小角落里，那地方正好遮掩他们的勾当。那里恰好有一块下沉的宽石板，与草皮齐平；青草从四边漫了上来。他们刮净了石板上的苔藓；碑文早已磨灭，只剩一个数字 7，是年月残存的一角。他们闲话的间隙，偶尔可以听见铜子儿敲在石头上那种叮叮的声响。

*的——的！*

「索普乡绅（Squire Thorpe）家里来客了，是不是？」赫奇斯问。这事他一清二楚，可他还想打听点别的，得绕着弯子够——乡下人就好这一手。

「啊；就是那个高个子，杰弗里·牛顿（Geoffrey Newton），还有瓦尔·布朗（Val Browne），开驯马场的那位。」

「那他准得要草了吧？」这是句引子，赫奇斯只管给猪搔背，装出一副毫不相干的样子。

===Original===
“I sold his trainer eighty ton o’ clauver,” said Ruck. “A’ be a gentleman, every inch of un.”

“Stiffish price, you?”

“Five pound ten.”

Whew!

“Ay, ay; but it be five mile to cart it; and a nation bad road.”

“What’s that long chap doing at Squire’s? He ’as been to Australia.”
===Chinese===
「俺卖给他那驯马师八十吨三叶草，」拉克说。「那人是个绅士，从头顶到脚后跟都是。」

「价钱不软和吧，恁说？」

「五镑十先令。」

嘿哟！

「可不，可不；可拉一趟五英里地；路况又烂得邪乎。」

「那个高个子在乡绅家干啥？他去过澳大利亚。」

===Original===
“A’ be goin’ to larn farming.”

“Larn farming!” Intense contempt.

“A’ be down to Greene Ferne a’ studying pretty often,” said Ruck, with a wink and a broad grin.

“Wimmen,” said Hedges, giving an extra hard scrape at the pig, who responded Humph﻿—humph!

“Wimmen,” repeated Ruck still more emphatically.

“There be worse thengs about,” said a voice behind. It was the clerk, who, having put the rector’s surplice ready, had slipped out for a minute into the churchyard to communicate a piece of news. He was a little shrivelled old fellow.
===Chinese===
「他是要学种庄稼。」

「学种庄稼！」一脸的鄙夷。

「他倒是常下格林费恩（Greene Ferne）去『用功』，」拉克说着，一挤眼，咧嘴一笑。

「娘们呗，」赫奇斯说，手上给猪搔得格外用力，猪回报以哼哧——哼哧！

「娘们，」拉克又强调了一遍，语气更重。

「世上还有更不像话的事哩，」背后有个声音说。是教堂执事，他把教区长的白法衣料理停当，抽空溜进墓园一分钟，来传达一条新闻。他是个干瘪的小老头。

===Original===
“Nash was allus a gay man,” said Ruck.

“So was his father afore un,” added Hedges. “It runs in the family.”

“Summut in the blood, summut in the blood,” said Nash, not to appear to value the hereditary propensity too highly. “Did ee never notice that shart men be a’most sure to get on with th’ wimmen? I got summut to tell ee.”

“What be it?” from both listeners at once.

If the Athenians were eager for something new, those that dwell in the fields are ten times more so.

“You knows Mr. Valentine Browne as built the new stables?”
===Chinese===
「纳什（Nash）向来是个风流人，」拉克说。

「他爹在他前头也是，」赫奇斯补一句。「祖传的。」

「血里带的，血里带的，」纳什说，倒不是要把自己这份祖传的癖性抬得多高。「恁们没留意过？矮个儿男人，十有八九跟娘们处得来。俺有条新闻要告诉恁们。」

「啥新闻？」两个听客异口同声。

雅典人尚且唯新是骛，住在田野里的人，热望新鲜事的热劲还要十倍于此。

「恁们认得瓦伦丁·布朗（Valentine Browne）先生吧，修新马场的那位？」

===Original===
“Sartainly.”

“He have took my cottage for the trout-fishing.”

“Aw! You calls un Hollyocks, doan’t ee?” said Ruck.

“A’ bean’t very far from Greene Ferne, be a’?” asked Hedges.

“Wimmen,” said the clerk meaningly. “ ’Pend upon it, it be the wimmen!”

“Lor, here um comes!” said Ruck.
===Chinese===
「认得认得。」

「他租了我那小屋，为着钓鳟鱼。」

「啊！就是叫『蜀葵小屋』（Hollyocks）的那个吧？」拉克说。

「离格林费恩不远吧？」赫奇斯问。

「娘们，」执事意味深长地说。「没错儿，准是为了娘们！」

「老天，说曹操曹操到！」拉克说。

===Original===
Two young men walked quickly round the tower, coming from the other side, down the gravel path past the group, and opening the wicket-gate went out into the field. Nash bowed and scraped, Ruck lifted his beaver, but neither seemed to observe these attentions.

“It be the wimmen, and no mistake,” said Ruck. “Thaay be gone to meet um. The Ferne folk be moast sure to come up thuck path this sunny day, ’stead of driving.”

“Marnin’, shepherd,” said the clerk to a labouring man who had just entered the churchyard. “I was afeared you’d be late. ’Spose you come from Upper Furlong. How’s your voice?”

“Aw, featish (fairish). I zucked a thrush’s egg to clear un.”

“Arl right, Jabez; mind as you doan’t zeng too fast. It be your fault, shepherd, it be your fault.”
===Chinese===
两个年轻人绕着教堂塔快步走来，从另一边过来，沿着砾石径走过这一群人，推开小门出了栅栏，到田里去了。纳什点头哈腰，拉克抬了抬他的海狸帽，可两位似乎谁也没把这些殷勤放在眼里。

「就是为了娘们，错不了，」拉克说。「他们这是接人去了。这么好的晴天，费恩那家人十有八九不坐车，要打那条小径走上来。」

「早啊，牧羊的，」执事对一个刚进墓园的做工的说。「俺还怕恁要迟到哩。俺猜恁是打上弗隆（Upper Furlong）来的。嗓子咋样？」

「啊，还成（featish，即 fairish）。俺吞了只鸫鸟蛋清了清嗓子。」

「这就好，杰贝兹（Jabez）；可当心别唱得太赶。都是恁的错，牧羊的，都是恁的错。」

===Original===
For Jabez was the leader of the choir. “Nash!” cried a stern voice, and the clerk jumped and tore his hat off at the sound. “Catch those boys!”

It was Squire Thorpe, whose magisterial eye had at once detected the youthful gamblers behind the buttress. Nash rushed towards them; but they had scented the Squire’s arrival, and dodged him round the big tombstones. Thorpe turned to the two farmers, who lifted their hats.

“Grass coming on nicely, Hedges,” said he. “Ought to be a good hay year.”

The Squire was as fond of gossip as any man in the parish; but he was rather late that morning; for he had hardly taken his stand by the wall when the *dill-dill* of the bell came to a sudden stop. The two gentlemen who had gone out into the field returned at a run.

“Ah, here you are!” said the Squire; and the three walked rapidly to the chancel door.
===Chinese===
原来杰贝兹是唱诗班的领唱。「纳什！」一声厉喝，执事吓得一蹦，闻声赶紧扯下帽子。「把那帮小子给我逮住！」

是索普乡绅，他那法官般的眼睛一眼就发现了扶壁后面那伙年少的小赌徒。纳什朝他们冲过去；可他们早嗅到了乡绅驾到，绕着大墓碑同他捉起了迷藏。索普转向两位农夫，两人举了举帽。

「草长得不赖呀，赫奇斯，」他说。「今年该是个好草年。」

论爱嚼舌根，乡绅不输教区里任何人；可那天早晨他来得迟了些；他刚在墙边站定，教堂的*的——的*声便戛然而止。方才出到田野里的那两位绅士，一路小跑了回来。

「啊，你们可来了！」乡绅说；三人快步向圣坛门走去。

===Original===
Ruck and Hedges, however, showed no signs of moving. A low hum arose from the hand-organ within; still they leant on the wall, deferring action to the last moment.

The sound of voices﻿—the speakers clearly almost out of breath, but none the less talking﻿—approached the wicket-gate, and three bonnets appeared above the wall there.

“It be the Greene Ferne folk,” said Hedges. “Measter Newton and t’other chap was too much in a hurry.”

Three ladies﻿—two young and one middle-aged﻿—entered the churchyard. The taller of the two girls left the path, and ran to a tomb enclosed with low iron railings. She carried a whole armful of spring flowers, gathered in the meadows and copses en route, bluebells and cowslips chiefly, and threw them broadcast on the grave.

“Miss Margaret don’t forget her feyther,” said Hedges.
===Chinese===
拉克和赫奇斯却毫无动身的表示。教堂里的手摇风琴嗡嗡低鸣起来；两人照旧倚着墙，能拖一刻是一刻。

一阵说话声——说的人分明已上气不接下气，却仍在说——朝小门逼近，墙头上露出了三顶女帽。

「是格林费恩那家人，」赫奇斯说。「牛顿先生和另外那位急得跟啥似的。」

三位女士——两位年轻的，一位中年的——走进了墓园。两个姑娘里高些的那个离开小径，跑到一座围着矮铁栏的墓前。她抱着满满一捧春花，是路上在草场和矮林里采的，多半是蓝铃花和黄花九轮草，一把把撒在坟上。

「玛格丽特（Margaret）小姐不忘她爹，」赫奇斯说。

===Original===
The three, as they passed, nodded smilingly to the two farmers, and went into the church.

“May Fisher be allus down at the Estcourts,” said Ruck. “S’pose her finds it dull up on the hills with the old man.”

“Mrs. Estcourt looks well,” said Hedges. “Warn hur’ll marry agen some day. Miss Margaret do dress a bit, you.”

“Nation gay. Hur be a upstanding girl, that Margaret Estcourt. A’ got a thousand pound under the will.”

“And the Greene Ferne Farm when the widder goes.”
===Chinese===
三人经过时，笑吟吟地朝两位农夫点头，进了教堂。

「梅·费舍尔（May Fisher）老在埃斯特科特家待着，」拉克说。「俺寻思她是嫌跟山上那老头子待着闷得慌。」

「埃斯特科特太太气色好，」赫奇斯说。「早晚有一天要再嫁。玛格丽特小姐穿戴是讲究了点儿，恁说。」

「时髦得很。玛格丽特·埃斯特科特（Margaret Estcourt），多挺拔的姑娘。遗嘱底下她得了一千镑。」

「还有格林费恩农场，等那寡妇一闭眼。」

===Original===
“Five hundred acres freehold, and them housen in to town.”

“A’ be a featish-looking girl, you.”

“So be May Fisher; but a’ bean’t such a queen as Mother. Margaret walks as if the parish belonged to her.”

“If a’ did, her would sell un, and buy a new bonnet. These yer wimmen!”

The sound of singing came from the open door under the tower hard by.
===Chinese===
「五百英亩自有地，外带城里那几处房。」

「长得标致，恁说。」

「梅·费舍尔也标致；可到底不如太太那样的王后气派。玛格丽特走起路来，倒像整个教区都是她家的。」

「真是她的，她准把教区卖了，买顶新帽子。这帮娘们！」

唱诗声从近旁塔下敞开的门里传出来。

===Original===
“Dall’d if it bean’t ‘I Will Arise.’ ”

“S’pose us had better go in.” They walked to the tower-door. It was arched and low﻿—so low that to enter it was necessary to stoop, and inside the pavement was a step beneath the level of the ground. Within stood the font, and by it some forms against the wall, on which the school children left their caps. There was a space behind the first pillar of the side-aisle unoccupied by pews, being dark and not affording a view of the pulpit. Now it was possible to tell the rank of the congregation as they entered, by the length of time each kept his hat on after getting through the door. The shepherd or carter took off his hat the moment he set his hobnailed boot down on the stone flags with a clatter. The wheelwright, who had a little money and a house of his own, wore his hat till he got to the font. So did the alehouse keeper, who had the grace to come to church. So did the small farmers. Ruck, who could write a cheque for a thousand pounds, never removed his till he arrived at the step that led down to the side-aisle. Hedges, who was higher in the rank of society, inasmuch as he had been born in the purple of farming, kept his on till he reached the first pillar. One of the semi-gentlemen-farmers actually walked halfway to his pew-door wearing his hat, though the congregation were standing listening to Jabez and the choir get through the introductory chant.

Entering from the beautiful sunshine, the church gave the impression of a rather superior tomb. It struck chilly, as if the cold of the last five or six centuries had got into it and could not be driven out. Cold rose up from the tombs under the aisles﻿—cold emanated from the walls, where slabs spoke of the dead﻿—cold came down from the very roof. Whitewashed walls, whitewashed pillars﻿—everything plain, bare, hard. The only colour to be seen was furnished by two small stained-glass windows, and the faded gilt and paint of the royal arms over the chancel; the lion and the unicorn in the middle, and the names of the churchwardens who reigned when it was put up on either side. The pews in the centre were modern; those in the side-aisles high, like boxes. There might, perhaps, have been forty people in the church altogether﻿—all crowded up towards the chancel: the back seats were quite empty. If a modest stranger went into such a back seat, and helped himself to the Prayerbook he might find there, the covers came off in his hand, and displayed a mass of sawdust-like borings thrown up by the grubs that had eaten their way right through the prayer for King William IV. A cheerless edifice﻿—tomb-like; and yet there were some to whom it had grown very dear in the passage of years, and others to whom it was equally dear because of associations. So it was that this chilly, harsh, repellent place﻿—squat rather than built on the edge of the hills﻿—was beloved far more by some of the worshippers therein than those grand vaulted cathedrals whose vastness seems to remove them from human sympathy. But how marked the contrast between the sunshine, the blue sky, the song of birds, the soft warm air, the green leaf and bud without!

Squire Thorpe’s pew of black oak occupied one entire side of the chancel; the choir and the barrel-organ were together, far down the side-aisle. From the raised dais of the chancel every member of the congregation could be discerned with ease. While the Rev. Basil Thorpe, cousin of the Squire, “droned in the pulpit,” or rather reading-desk, the Squire, sitting, kneeling, or standing, surveyed with keen glance every nook and corner. This severe and continuous examination did not in the least interfere with his devotions. Such is the dual character of the mind, that he uttered the responses earnestly in his sonorous tones, and at the same moment noted the two wenches giggling with the ploughboy behind the pillar. His imagination followed the lesson and saw the patriarchal life on the plains of Chaldea, while his physical eye watched the grey-haired “forefather” in his blue smock-frock, who, leaning his chin upon his ashen staff, traced the words with his horny finger on the book. The school children sat on forms placed endwise down the centre aisle. He saw one near the top stealthily produce an apple, and after taking a bite hand it to the next. All down the row it went, each nibbling in turn, and the final receiver putting the core in his pocket. Such innocent tricks did not annoy him in the least﻿—his mind was broad enough to make allowances for the little weaknesses of human nature﻿—the one thing that hurt him was the empty pews. He looked to see who was absent. He knew every inhabitant of the parish, and as it were checked them off mentally. It was a process he went through every Sunday with the same depressing result. The church was practically deserted: he hardly dared own to himself how small was the percentage that attended. Now the Squire felt no animosity against Bethel Chapel﻿—he was candid enough to own that Basil was dull in the pulpit and somewhat mistaken in the tone of his intercourse with the poor. Still, to desert the church was as if a man turned his back on his own father, and preferred to sit at a stranger’s hearth. He could not help associating it with that general divorce, as it were, of the people from authority, the general contempt for property and capital, the loss of respect for institutions of all kinds, that is so striking a feature of modern English life. Then his gaze fell on the group of three ladies in a high old-fashioned pew, and he marked Margaret’s bonnet.

“Another,” was his thought﻿—“another since last week. But she is singularly handsome, and so like her poor father.” And his gaze grew gentle, noting the empty corner of the pew where the stalwart frame of his oldest friend had sat till darkness closed the eye of the boldest of riders and keenest of shots. Involuntarily he looked across at the marble tablet on the opposite wall of the chancel﻿—set there at his own special wish﻿—and read:
===Chinese===
「该死，唱的这不是《我要起来》嘛。」

「俺们还是进去吧。」两人朝塔门走去。门是拱的，又矮——矮得进门非弯腰不可，门里的地面还比外面低一级。门内立着洗礼盆，旁边靠墙摆着几条长凳，学堂的孩子们把帽子撂在那上面。侧廊第一根柱子后头有一块没装座椅的空地，又暗，又瞧不见讲坛。这时，凭各人进门后戴帽子的时长，已能判断会众的身份高低。牧羊人或车夫，钉着平头钉的靴子一踏上石板地、咔哒一响，帽子便摘了。轮匠手头有几个钱、有自己的房，帽子要戴到洗礼盆跟前。肯来教堂的酒馆老板也一样。小农户们也一样。拉克拉得出一张一千镑的支票，帽子却要戴到通侧廊的台阶前才摘。赫奇斯呢，社会身份更高些——他生在务农世家的「紫衣」里——要走到第一根柱子跟前才脱帽。有位半绅士农户，竟然戴着帽子走到了自家座位门口一半的路程——那时全体会众都站着，听杰贝兹和唱诗班唱完进堂咏。

从明媚的阳光里走进来，这教堂给人的印象，是一座相当上等的坟墓。一股寒气袭人，仿佛近五六个世纪的阴冷钻进了它的骨头，再也驱赶不出。寒气从侧廊底下的坟墓里升上来——寒气从嵌着墓碑、诉说着死者的墙壁里渗出来——寒气从屋顶直压下来。刷白的墙，刷白的柱——一切都素净、空硬。仅有的颜色来自两扇小小的彩色玻璃窗，和圣坛上方褪色的王家纹章的金漆彩绘；当中是狮子和独角兽，两侧是它立起来那年在任的教堂守人的名字。正中的座席是现代的；侧廊里的又高又深，像一只只匣子。整座教堂里兴许有四十个人——全挤在靠圣坛的一头：后排的座位空空荡荡。若有个腼腆的生客坐进那样的后排，拿起手边可能搁着的公祷书，书皮立刻散在他手里，露出一堆锯末似的蛀屑——蛀虫一直啃穿了威廉四世（King William IV）的祷文。一座冷清的建筑——坟茔一般；然而岁月流转，有些人已对它生出深深的依恋，另一些人则因种种牵念同样爱它。于是，这座冷峭、粗粝、拒人的屋子——与其说建在、不如说蹲踞在丘陵边缘——反倒比那些穹顶巍峨的大教堂，更得其中一些礼拜者的深爱；大教堂的宏阔，似乎使它们远离了人间的温情。可同外面的阳光、蓝天、鸟语、暖风、绿叶和蓓蕾相比，反差何其强烈！

索普乡绅的黑橡木座位占着圣坛整整一侧；唱诗班和铁皮风琴在一起，远在侧廊那头。从圣坛高起的台上一眼望去，会众个个看得清楚。当牧师大人巴兹尔·索普（Basil Thorpe）——乡绅的堂兄——「在讲坛上哼经」，或者说在读经台上哼经的时候，乡绅或坐或跪或立，锐利的目光扫视着每个角落。这场严厉而不间断的检查，丝毫不妨碍他的敬虔。人心便是如此具有两重性：他以洪亮的嗓音恳切地应答着，同一瞬间又盯上了柱子后头同小犁童咯咯傻笑的两个丫头。他的想象力随经文而去，看见了迦勒底平原上的先民生活；他的肉眼却望着蓝罩衫里白发的「老祖宗」，那人下巴拄着白蜡木杖，正用角质的手指在书上逐字描着。学堂的孩子们坐在中廊里首尾相接的长凳上。他看见前排一个悄悄掏出一只苹果，咬了一口递给下一个。苹果顺着整排传下去，一个个轮流啃，最后接到的人把核揣进了兜里。这样天真的小把戏一点也不叫他恼——他的胸怀宽宏，容得下人性的小小弱点——唯一刺痛他的，是那些空座位。他挨个查看缺了谁。教区里的每一个居民他都认得，仿佛在心里逐一点卯。这套功课他每个礼拜天都要做一遍，回回是同样叫人丧气的结果。教堂实际上被荒弃了：来礼拜的人比例小到什么程度，他几乎不敢对自己承认。乡绅对贝瑟尔礼拜堂（Bethel Chapel）并无宿怨——他也坦率承认，巴兹尔讲道沉闷，同穷人打交道的调门也颇有偏差。可尽管如此，背弃教堂，就如同一个人掉头不理自己的父亲，偏要坐到陌生人的炉边。他不由得把这事同那种民与官长整个离了心的局面——同对产业与资本的普遍轻蔑、对一切体制敬意的丧失——联系起来；那正是现代英格兰生活里触目惊心的一幕。随后他的目光落在高高的老式座位里三位女士身上，他留意到了玛格丽特的帽子。

「又一顶，」他想——「打上礼拜起又是一顶。可她真是出奇地美，又那么像她可怜的父亲。」他的目光温柔下来，落到座位那空着的一角——他最老的朋友那魁梧的身躯曾在那里落座，直到黑暗合上了这位最无畏的骑手、最敏锐的枪手的眼睛。他不由自主地望向对面圣坛墙上的大理石碑——那是他特意嘱咐立在那里的——读道：

===Original===
> **To The Beloved Memory 

 of 

 Warren Estcourt.**

The black lettering on the pure white marble grew dim; his eyes became misty. Then came the sorrowful, and yet assured, prayer:
===Chinese===
> **谨以挚爱纪念

　献给

　沃伦·埃斯特科特（Warren Estcourt）。**

纯白大理石上黑色的字迹模糊了；他的眼睛蒙上了雾。接着响起了那悲恸而又笃定的祷文：

===Original===
“Make them to be numbered with Thy saints: in glory everlasting.”

Rude voices chanted it﻿—voices used to the roaring of the wind in the trees and the hiss of the rain on the hill. Yet as they stood there and gave forth the old, old words that have been linked with human fate so many centuries, there came a meaning through the hoarse harsh notes.

A tear fell on the broad yellow page of the old Prayerbook the Squire held so closely to his face. This was why the low grey church was so dear to him; it was full of the past. Shadowy forms hid behind the pillars; faces looked down from the worm-eaten rafters; bright and yet quick-fading groups of other days appeared through the greenish-yellow panes of the windows.

“I am an old fool,” he said to himself. “If these young fellows see me, they will laugh.”

But the young fellows by his side were otherwise engaged. They too had noted the extravagant bonnet; but their thoughts went no farther than the face beneath it. The old man thought of the father; the young men of the living daughter. But, indeed, Margaret Estcourt could not but be observed, standing so manifestly apart from, and yet among, that simple congregation. A single flower in a gloomy room will sometimes light it up as with a glory﻿—the eye instantly rests upon it; a single violet will fill the place with perfume. She was the violet in that ancient building. Yet there was nothing extraordinary about her﻿—no marvellous hyacinthine or golden tresses, no burning eye flashing with southern passion. She was simply very near the ideal of a fair young English girl, in the full glow of youth and with all its exquisite bloom. Perhaps at the first glance the beautiful pose of the tall and graceful figure seemed her most distinctive characteristic. The slight form of May Fisher brought her still more into relief.
===Chinese===
「求你使他们得列于你的圣徒之中：享受永远的荣耀。」

粗粝的嗓音唱着它——那是听惯了树间风吼、山上雨啸的嗓子。然而当他们站在那里，唱出这千百年来同人类命运相连的古老古老的字句时，嘶哑粗厉的音符里透出了一种意义。

一滴泪落在乡绅紧贴着脸的那本旧公祷书宽大的黄纸页上。这便是这座低矮的灰教堂对他如此珍贵的缘故；它满是往昔。幢幢人影藏在柱子后面；一张张面孔从虫蛀的椽木上俯视下来；昔日的、鲜明而又倏忽即逝的人群，从窗户的黄绿色玻璃里浮现出来。

「我是个老糊涂，」他自言自语。「叫这两个年轻人瞧见，该笑话我了。」

可他身旁的两个年轻人正另有心事。那顶奢华的帽子他们也注意到了；但他们的心思没有超出帽子底下的那张脸。老人想的是父亲；年轻人想的是活着的女儿。的确，玛格丽特·埃斯特科特站在那朴素的会众之中、又分明超乎其上，想不引人注目也难。阴暗的房间里，一朵花有时会给它满室生辉——目光立刻便落在它身上；一朵紫罗兰会使满室生香。她便是那座古老建筑里的紫罗兰。然而她身上并没有什么惊人之处——没有风信子般的乌发或金发，没有闪烁着南方热情的灼灼明眸。她只是非常接近一位白皙美丽的年轻英国少女的理想典范，正当青春的光焰，带着青春全部的娇艳。或许第一眼望去，那高挑优雅身姿的美妙仪态，最算是她独有的特色。梅·费舍尔纤小的身影，把她衬托得益发分明。

===Original===
So to the young men in the chancel pew the old grey church began to grow very dear, and the first part of the service slipped by speedily, despite Basil’s droning. Then the choir gave out a hymn with all their might, sturdily drowning the organ, stimulated to an extra effort by the presence of visitors in the Squire’s pew. Jabez the shepherd sang like a giant refreshed with wine, and got through four verses magnificently. But in the triumph of the moment he forgot the clerk’s warning not to “zeng too fast.” The verse finished with the word “Jacob:” Jabez unfortunately got a little in advance of the time, and desperately struggling to lengthen it out, an alehouse chorus slipped from his tongue: “Ja-aa-fol-de-rol-cob!”

When Basil went up into the pulpit the Squire quietly folded a silk handkerchief as a cushion to protect the back of his head from the hard oak of the pew, and slumbered peacefully till at the Rector’s “Fifthly, and in conclusion,” the stir of relief that ran through the congregation awoke him, as it usually did. Thorpe waited in the churchyard for Mrs. Estcourt, and walked with her as far as the wicket-gate, his own carriage waiting at the road entrance. There at the wicket the group paused a moment on the edge of the green fields. The sweetness of the air coming from the Downs, after the close and yet chilly atmosphere of the church, was in itself an exquisite pleasure. The larks still sang, the sun still shone, and the clouds came over the hill. Yet there was something more beautiful still in the mantling colour of Margaret’s soft check. Youth and love﻿—youth and love and May-time!

*Cuckoo﻿—cuckoo!* from the bird on the elm below the hill.

“O, look!” said Mrs. Estcourt, suddenly, in some alarm, yet laughing. “My poor shepherd”﻿—for Jabez worked for her. Wildly he fled over grassy grave and tombstone, chased by a mob of smock-frocks and boys yelling “Fol-de-rol-cob!” at his heels, till, reaching the wall, he leaped, hitched his toe in the coping, and fell prone among the docks and nettles and pigs.

The Squire laughed heartily. By-and-by, as he leaned back in his carriage, the thought came into his mind that this was human life in little. First, pathos in the memory of his old friend; next, love﻿—for he shrewdly suspected Geoffrey and Val﻿—and beside that love the grim tomb and sad low mound; finally, the grotesque. Wherefore the old monks, seeing that all life came to the church in their day, carved fantastic faces on the gargoyles grinning down, sneering and mocking at it. And even sweet young love brought its regret. “For,” thought he to himself, as he narrowly scanned the faces of the young men sitting opposite, “I fancy I detect a coldness and distance already between these boys, who used to be more than brothers. Margaret has come between them.”
===Chinese===
就这样，对圣坛座位里的两个年轻人来说，这座灰色的老教堂也开始变得亲切起来；礼拜的前半场不知不觉便滑了过去，任凭巴兹尔哼他的经。接着唱诗班使出全力唱起一首圣歌，结结实实地盖过了风琴——乡绅席上来了生客，给他们添了一把额外的劲。牧羊人杰贝兹唱得像个痛饮过的大汉，气势磅礴地唱完了四节。可是得意的当口，他忘了执事的告诫，别「唱得太赶」。那一节的末一个词是「雅各」（Jacob）：杰贝兹不幸抢了一点儿拍，拼命想把这个词拖长，一句酒馆里的大合唱却溜出了他的舌头：「雅——啊——福多罗科布！」

巴兹尔登上讲坛时，乡绅不声不响折起一方丝手帕当垫子，护住后脑别磕着座位的硬橡木，安然睡去，直到教区长那声「第五点，作为总结」，会众如释重负的骚动照例把他唤醒。索普在墓园里等埃斯特科特太太，陪她走到小门跟前，他自家的马车在大路口候着。就在小门那里，一行人在绿色田野的边缘伫立了片刻。教堂里那又闷又冷的空气之后，丘原上吹来的甜美清风，本身便是一桩精妙的享受。云雀还在唱，太阳还在照，云朵正翻过山头。然而还有更美的——玛格丽特脸颊上泛起的那抹红晕。青春与爱——青春、爱与五月天！

*布谷——布谷！*山下榆树上的鸟儿叫着。

「哎呀，快瞧！」埃斯特科特太太突然喊道，又惊又笑。「我那可怜的牧羊人」——杰贝兹是给她干活的。他没命地奔过青草萋萋的坟头和墓碑，身后一群罩衫汉子和孩子追着，脚跟后齐声喊「福多罗科布！」，直追到他跑到墙边，纵身一跃，脚尖绊在墙压顶上，扑面栽进了酸模、荨麻和猪堆里。

乡绅开怀大笑。过了一阵，他靠在马车座背上，心里忽然想到：这便是人生的一个缩影。先是伤感，在对老友的追忆里；接着是爱——他精明地猜到了杰弗里和瓦尔——而在爱旁边，是森然的墓和凄凉的低冢；末了，是滑稽。所以从前的修士们，眼见他们那个时代形形色色的人生尽数来到教堂，便在滴水兽上刻下奇形怪状的鬼脸，朝下咧嘴而笑，冷笑着，嘲弄着它。就连甜美的少年之爱，也带来了它的憾恨。「因为，」他细细端详对面两张年轻的脸，暗自想道，「我看这两个小伙子之间，已经有点冷淡和隔阂了——他们本来亲得胜过兄弟。是玛格丽特插在了他们中间。」
